IT IS NOT MISYN-S AUTOGRAPH. 
will be observed that the colopnon, p. 104, etatea distinctly that The 
Fire of Love waa translated by Richard Misyn and "^er dictum frairem 
Ricardum Misyn scriptum ^ coivectum." Belying upon this, Coxe'a Cata^ 
log and Tanner's Eihliotheea say that it is in Miayn's hand. Now, the 
writing throughout the ■volume ie in the same hand, eo that if Miayn wrote 
Tlie Fire of Loee, he also wrote The Mendinri of Life. We ate, however, 
here met by a serious difficulty, for, whereas the colophons on pp. 68 and 
104 are dated 1435 a.d., the colophon on p. 131 is dated 1434 a.d. This, 
it must be observed, is not due to a binder's error, for the colophon of Fire 
of Love, Bk. II, is on the same sheet of vellum as the beginning of TJie 
Mending of Life. Now, an author could scarcely bo guilty of putting 
1434 A.D. after 1435 a.d., unless, having written The Fire in 1435, ha 
proceeded to copy a translation of Tlie Mending done the year before. This 
is the only supposition which, in face of the colophons, will allow ua to 
regard the MS. as in Miayn'a own hand. It is a possible supposition, but 
scarcely a probable one. It is much more natural to suppose that a scribe 
has copied the whole MS., including colophons, and bas left no trace of hia 
own identity. An examination of the text will show this supposition not 
only rational, hut practically a certain fact. ^m 

IT IS NOT A CONSISTENT TEXT. ^| 

A careful reading of the MS. brings to light a number of mistakes, not 
of any great importance, but fortunately, I think, quite sufficient to establish 
the fact of its being a copyist's work, and unfortunately, quite enough to 
detract from its second great claim to importance, viz. as a dated and 
consistent dialectic text. 

Of these mistakes (which are exactly the kind of mistakes we look for 
in copyists' works) 1 give some of the more important, hut the reader will 
do well to make a caid'ul study of the text himself.

A FEW MISTAKES EXAMINED. 

If we examine the word resonalnl, struck out on p. 26, we shall see that 
it ia clearly due to the word rmmainjll occmring immediately hefore ; but wo 
alao notice there ia a difference in the spelling! Wliy ia this difEorenee! 
Probably because in one case the scribe copies Misyn'e spelling, and in the 
other case he spells according to his own system. Which then is the copy 
of Misyn t I should say the form resonabyll, because the ^ and the double 
consonant are more consistent with the remainder of the MS., and because 
the scribe wonld be more likely to write down the copied form first, and 
then, writing from memory, and probably in a fit of abstraction, he puts 
down bis ovm form. So too we find chastis (98/1) due to chaetys in the line 
before. 

These two examples would be almost unworthy of mention, were it not 
that they seem to supply us with a key to the very serious irregularities of 
spelling throughout the work. Taking what was, probably, an already 
ineonsistently-spellcil MS., the copyist has in many cases, from carelessness, 
introduced his own spelling. Thus, side by side with the strong Northern 
guttural in gichilk, qicediji; we find sometimes the softer whilk, ichedijr, Ac. 
While, too, the roll of the r ia usually emphasized by an additional e or 5, as 
in mtn-yf (serve) ; qivJiarefore, we find, too, irharfor and wharfore. hrynnynge 
and the softer fonn bimynffe are found almost equally. So, too, ijiily occurs 
(44/30), but the more usual form is treicly or trmJy. 

The broken vowels alao supply us with good proofs of the mixture of 
dialects. Generally speaking, an 0,E. long vowel is represented hy a broken 
vowel, and an O.E. broken vowel by a simple vowel representing the fivd 
element of the older broken vowel. Thus we constantly read forsoilli , ffof- 
soyth, foyl (fool), dnyTie (done), BOyne (soon), poyr (poor), &c, and for tlie 
older &} ea, we read mric (work) werk; dyrlcnes (from dsorcnea), lese (lose) 
from leoean. But wo abo I'cad occasionally, side by side with these, forsoth 



I (-27129, 3i/38), fformtli (41/30), /ole (19/28), foles (2G/10), /ore, fone, don ; 

' and again lom (45/34), warh. 

All these more exceptional forms, with the single exception perhaps of 
Kark, clearly point to a more Southern and more inland dialect. 

"We shall, therefore, prohalily not be far ivrong if we put down the 

1 present MS. as copied from Miayu's translation Ly a ntcribe coming poaeibly 

I from Ely or Peterborough. 

NOTE ON LETTER y. 
Dr. rnmivall kindly reminds me that the broken vowel oy for o is Old 
Yorkshire, oceniring constantly in the Towncleij Myderieg [R K T. S. 1897]. 
I am not quite aore whether the vowel of the modern Yorkshire rtoU [coat, 
01 as in hoW] la a true descendant of this oy. Probably however the broken 
Towel in hoan (bone), hoiit (boat), &c., is a descendant, and we certainly have 
wioiw or moo'in (moon), foU or fooU (fool), mm or aoom (soon). These examples 
probably give ub almost the exact vowel pronunciation of eoi/fh, foyl, aoyn in 
our MS. 0£ course in the MS. under consideration the difficulty lies in the 
changing value of y. In sicyft, lyft and such forms we have the full short sound 
1 ; but more frequently it is used merely aa a connective between two sounds 
to give a force to the one which caused the scribe some difficulty to express. 
This leads to the very extraordinary form mtryff or sanjf (serve) — a form 
which I cannot find elsewhere. The scribe is however simply trying to ex- 
press a very strong rolling r, and so pronouncing the whole 'sarSf,' giving 
the y the soimd of the atonic e in ' several.' [See Key, Pron. H. E. D.] We 
might comp, with this the full trill of the original r in woruld, and the modern 
vocalised r in world. The same remarks apply to the medial e in qtcharefoi-e. 

GEAJrMAE. 

The accidence is of the slightest kind, and can cause the reader no 
difficulty, A glance down the accompanying list of verbs will show that it 
is quite impossible to draw up a paradigm. There are no Southern pi, forms 
in efh, or Midland pi. in en. 

The order of words is un-English, and generally follows the Latin fairly 
closely. Still the un-English order seems to be due to the translator being 
thoroughly accustomed to Latin order, and perhaps thinking in Latin order, 
riither than to his careful adherence to the text before him. For some- 
times, when the Latin order is unclassical and like English, the translator 

8 not follow the Latin version, but writes in the order we should stpect 
to iind in the Latin test, thus : —

RICHAED MISYN. 

Beyond what we find in the colophons at the end of the books of these 
MSS., nothing can be found with any certainty concerning this Eichard 




Iidroditctii/n. 

SiUyn. "So iccoid of him cxistfi at Lincoln ; and the furthest we can go, is 
to say that he was possibly the Kicardus Myeyn mentioned in 1461-2 in 
' The Eegiater of the Guild of Corpus Christi in the City of York.' Surtees 
Soc, 1872, p. 62:— 

" Nomina Fratnim et Sotorum admissorum per Domtnum Johannem 
Jiiii'ton, BectoTem ecclesiai Hancti Martini in Mikelgale, et suos consortes, 
Anno Domini iniUeaimo CCCC"° LXJ° [1461-2]. 

Per Aorainum Johannem Burton, secundum magistram nostra gildas. 

In primis, frater 'BAcardus Mysyn, BtiSrc^enus, ordinis Fm,tram Car 
meiitanim . . . ." 

An earlier bishop of the same name ia mentiond on Uie tim of Archhp. 
Scrope's Indulgence Cup, ib. 291 n, 292 n. See Poole and Hugall'a Hut. 
Mui Descriptive Guide to York Cathedral, p. 197 : 

" 4- Becbardo arche beschope Scrope gtantea on to oU thu that drinkis 
of this cope xl'' dayis to pardun. Bobeit Gubsun. Beechope Musln grantea 
in same forme afore saide xl" dayis to pardun. Itobert StensalL" 

'Beschope Musin' was probably Eichard Messing, who, according to 
Cotton (Fasti Ecd. Hih., iii. 277) was bishop of Dromore from 1408 to 
1410, Another bishop of the same name was admitted into the Guild in 
1461-2, as " Frater Etc. Mysyn, suffragenus, ordims Fratrum Carmelitanun." 
See p. 62, aTiiea. 

As to Miayn's Friary at Lincoln, Dugdale says, Momisticon (ed. 1830), 
vi. 1571, col. 2:— 

[Carmelite or] White Friars, at Lincoln.^ — Lelaud, in his Itimrary, vol. 
i., pp. 32, 33, has one or two sentences relating to the "Wbite Friara at 
Lincoln. Their House, it appears, was situated on the West side of the 
High-street, in the lower part of the Town called Wikerford. " Gualterus," 
he says, "as I hard, caullid Dorotheus, Dene of Lincoln, a Scottish man," 
was " first Founder of the White Friers in Lincoln," Speed saya thia 
House was founded by Odo de Ivilkenny, a Scot, a.d. 1269. The Site was 
j,Tanted in the 36th Hen. Vlllth to John Broxholm.'^ The Particular for it 
ia in the Augmentation Office.
